Step 03 — Busy wait (broken).

Lab 03 / Step 03 — Busy wait (broken)

Track: device-labs · Project: labs/device-labs

broken on purpose

rbdev run 03
# step switch in ex03-reaction-game.c:  ex03_step03_start

Source: labs/device-labs/ex03-reaction-game/steps/step-03-busy-wait/README.md

Device lab: Reaction Game
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p
Depends on: Step 02

1. What this step must deliver

  • Same game flow as step 02.
  • WAIT uses a busy-spin until the deadline.
  • You should notice the UI is stuck / device feels “dead” during WAIT.

Success: you can name why spinning is worse than vTaskDelay.

2. Problems this step faces

  • Spinning until a tick deadline uses 100% CPU and never yields.
  • No chance to refresh a “still waiting…” hint.

3. Design approach (intentionally bad)

ChoiceWhy (for the lesson)
Busy while until deadlineLooks like “wait until time T”; starves UI

Architecture

┌──────────┐  set WAIT  ┌──────────┐  spin      ┌─────────┐
│   ui     │ ─────────► │ deadline │ ──────────►│  tick   │
│  loop    │            │  loop    │  no yield  │ compare │
└──────────┘            └──────────┘            └─────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
                        banner frozen / CPU hot

State machine

┌────────┐   BOOT   ┌────────┐   spin done   ┌────────┐
│ READY  │ ───────► │  WAIT  │ ────────────► │   GO   │
└────────┘          │ (busy) │               └────────┘
                    └────────┘

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 03  |  Step: ex03.c
rbdev run 03   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex03.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

wait_ms = 1000u + (rnd_u32() % 2000u);
react_set_state(REACT_ST_WAIT);
react_set_detail("BUSY wait %u", (unsigned)wait_ms);
react_set_hint("banner freezes");

deadline = xTaskGetTickCount() + pdMS_TO_TICKS(wait_ms);
/*
 * INTENTIONAL BUG: spin until deadline — no yield.
 * UI cannot refresh; other tasks starve. Do not ship this.
 */
while (xTaskGetTickCount() < deadline) {
    spin++;
}

go_tick = xTaskGetTickCount();
react_set_state(REACT_ST_GO);
/* ... BOOT edge + RESULT ms same as step 02 ... */

How the code works

Same round shape as step 02 until WAIT.

The bug.
Instead of vTaskDelay(wait_ms), the code computes a future deadline and spins in a tight while reading xTaskGetTickCount(). That loop never calls a blocking FreeRTOS API, so this task stays Running and the banner cannot show a live hint.

After the spin.
GO / BOOT / RESULT are unchanged — the lesson is only the wait strategy.

Contrast.
Step 02’s vTaskDelay at least yielded (banner still “frozen” because blocked, but polite). Step 04’s timer lets the UI keep pulsing during WAIT.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — delay vs busy-wait

ApproachYields?UI during WAITCPU
vTaskDelay (step 02)YesFrozen because blocked, but politeLow
Busy-spin (this step)NoFrozen + hostileHigh
Timer (step 04)YesCan stay liveLow
MythReality
“I’m just waiting for a tick”Without block/yield you monopolize the CPU
“FreeRTOS failed”Architecture chose a spin

7. Checkpoint

  • Felt / saw freeze during WAIT
  • Can point at the while (tick < deadline) bug in the sketch
  • Can contrast spin vs vTaskDelay vs timer

8. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
No obvious freezeUse a long wait (~3 s); watch hint stuck on “banner freezes”
Device resetsWDT may trip on long spin — that is the lesson

Step 04 — Timer GO.

Lab 03 / Step 04 — Timer GO

Track: device-labs · Project: labs/device-labs

rbdev run 03
# step switch in ex03-reaction-game.c:  ex03_step04_start

Source: labs/device-labs/ex03-reaction-game/steps/step-04-timer-go/README.md

Device lab: Reaction Game
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p
Depends on: Step 03

1. What this step must deliver

  • Random WAIT started with a one-shot software timer.
  • During WAIT the hint line keeps pulsing (live N) — UI stays alive.
  • Timer callback only xTaskNotifyGives the UI task (short!).
  • GO → BOOT → RESULT ms as before.

Success: watch live count up during WAIT, then GO appears.

2. Problems this step faces

  • Need “fire GO later” without blocking the UI task.
  • Timer callbacks run in the timer service task — must stay short.

3. Design approach

ChoiceWhy
xTimerCreate(..., pdFALSE, …)One-shot (does not auto-reload)
xTimerChangePeriod + xTimerStartSet random wait, then arm
Callback → xTaskNotifyGive(g_ui)Wake UI; no LCD / beep in callback
UI polls notify with short timeoutCan update hint while waiting

Architecture

┌──────────┐   Start    ┌──────────┐   Notify   ┌──────────┐
│ ui task  │ ─────────► │ go timer │ ─────────► │ ui task  │
└──────────┘             └──────────┘             └──────────┘

The ui task keeps pulsing react_* hints on the LCD during WAIT.

Sequence

ui                 go timer           tmr svc
 |                     |                    |
 |-- ChangePeriod ---->|                    |
 |-- Start ----------->|                    |
 |-- pulse hint        |                    |
 |                     |-- fire ----------->|
 |                     |                    |-- NotifyGive
 |<- GO wake ----------|                    |
 |-- show GO           |                    |

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 03  |  Step: ex03.c
rbdev run 03   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex03.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

static TaskHandle_t g_ui;
static TimerHandle_t g_go_timer;
static TickType_t g_go_tick;

static void go_timer_cb(TimerHandle_t t)
{
    (void)t;
    g_go_tick = xTaskGetTickCount();
    if (g_ui != NULL) {
        (void)xTaskNotifyGive(g_ui);  /* SHORT — no LCD / beep */
    }
}

static void ui_task(void *arg)
{
    /* ... READY + BOOT to start ... */
    wait_ms = 1000u + (rnd_u32() % 2000u);
    react_set_state(REACT_ST_WAIT);
    (void)xTimerChangePeriod(g_go_timer, pdMS_TO_TICKS(wait_ms), 0);
    (void)xTimerStart(g_go_timer, 0);

    (void)ulTaskNotifyTake(pdTRUE, 0); /* clear stale */
    for (;;) {
        if (ulTaskNotifyTake(pdTRUE, pdMS_TO_TICKS(20)) > 0u) {
            break; /* timer fired → GO */
        }
        react_set_hint("live %u", ++pulse);
    }

    react_set_state(REACT_ST_GO);
    /* ... BOOT edge + RESULT ms ... */
}

void app_main(void)
{
    bsp_init();
    g_go_timer = xTimerCreate("go", pdMS_TO_TICKS(1000), pdFALSE, NULL, go_timer_cb);
    (void)xTaskCreate(ui_task, "ui", 4096, NULL, 5, &g_ui);
}

How the code works

app_main.
Creates a one-shot timer (pdFALSE = no auto-reload) and the UI task. Save the UI handle in g_ui so the callback can notify it.

go_timer_cb.
Runs in the timer service task. Only stamps g_go_tick and xTaskNotifyGive(g_ui). Never touches the LCD or beep_play.

Starting WAIT.
xTimerChangePeriod sets the random delay; xTimerStart arms it. UI does not call vTaskDelay(wait_ms).

WAIT loop.
ulTaskNotifyTake with a short timeout: if the count is > 0, GO time arrived; if it times out, refresh live N and poll again. That is why the hint keeps moving during WAIT.

GO / RESULT.
Same tick math as step 02 once the notify wakes the UI.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — software timers

Why a timer (not delay in UI)

vTaskDelay in UIOne-shot timer
Who waitsThe UI taskTimer service
UI during waitBlockedCan poll / draw
“Do X later”Awkward if UI must stay liveNatural

Key APIs

APIRole
xTimerCreate(name, period, pdFALSE, id, cb)Create one-shot timer
xTimerChangePeriodSet next wait length
xTimerStartArm it
CallbackRuns in timer daemon — NotifyGive only

Callback rules

Allowed: notify, give semaphore, set a flag carefully.
Not allowed here: beep_play, long loops, complex UI.

Mental model

Delay = this task sleeps.
Timer = ask the system to wake someone later.

7. Checkpoint

  • live increments during WAIT
  • Can explain one-shot + short callback
  • Can point at ChangePeriod / Start / NotifyTake in the sketch
  • Reaction ms still works

8. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
Never GOxTimerStart / ChangePeriod missing
Crash in waitBeep/LCD moved into callback — undo
No pulseNotifyTake timeout too long / loop broken

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 03  |  Step: ex03.c
rbdev run 03   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex03.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

static TaskHandle_t g_ui;
static TaskHandle_t g_audio;
static TimerHandle_t g_go_timer;
static uint32_t g_best_ms = 0xffffffffu;

static void go_timer_cb(TimerHandle_t t)
{
    g_go_tick = xTaskGetTickCount();
    (void)xTaskNotifyGive(g_ui);  /* SHORT — no beep here */
}

static void audio_task(void *arg)
{
    for (;;) {
        (void)ulTaskNotifyTake(pdTRUE, portMAX_DELAY);
        (void)beep_play(BEEP_CLICK, NULL);
    }
}

/* inside ui WAIT loop: */
for (;;) {
    if (ulTaskNotifyTake(pdTRUE, pdMS_TO_TICKS(20)) > 0u) {
        break; /* real GO */
    }
    if (boot_pressed(&boot_was)) {
        false_start = 1;
        (void)xTimerStop(g_go_timer, 0);
        break;
    }
    react_set_hint("live %u", ++pulse);
}

if (false_start) {
    react_set_state(REACT_ST_RESULT);
    react_set_detail("FALSE START");
    continue;
}

react_set_state(REACT_ST_GO);
(void)xTaskNotifyGive(g_audio);  /* wake audio — do not beep in UI */

/* after BOOT on GO: */
ms = (xTaskGetTickCount() - g_go_tick) * portTICK_PERIOD_MS;
if (ms < g_best_ms) {
    g_best_ms = ms;
}

void app_main(void)
{
    bsp_init();
    g_go_timer = xTimerCreate("go", pdMS_TO_TICKS(1000), pdFALSE, NULL, go_timer_cb);
    (void)xTaskCreate(audio_task, "audio", 4096, NULL, 5, &g_audio);
    (void)xTaskCreate(ui_task, "ui", 4096, NULL, 5, &g_ui);
}

How the code works

Timer callback. Still only stamps g_go_tick and NotifyGives the UI — never plays sound.

WAIT loop. Each poll either gets the GO notify, sees an early BOOT (false start → xTimerStop), or refreshes the live hint.

GO. UI notifies audio_task; audio alone calls beep_play(BEEP_CLICK). Then BOOT scores ms and maybe updates g_best_ms.

app_main. Create the one-shot timer, then audio first (so g_audio is valid), then UI.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — timer + notify together

ToolJob
Software timerSchedule GO
Notify (timer→UI)“GO time” event
Notify (UI→audio)“Play click” event

No new primitive beyond step 04’s timer — step 05 is composition and game rules. Queue still unused on purpose.

7. Checkpoint

  • False start works
  • Best time updates
  • Click on GO; UI stays live in WAIT
  • Can name Lab 03’s 3 FreeRTOS features

8. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
No soundAudio task / g_audio / volume
GO after false startMust xTimerStop on early press
PWR does nothing usefulUse BOOT

Success: steady visual pulse at ~120 BPM.

  • Need a clear metronome face before audio/timing lessons.

ChoiceWhy
metro_* helperOwns layout

Architecture

One task owns the metronome face; each period is flash, short hold, idle, then sleep the remainder.

┌─────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐
│ metro_flash │ ──► │ vTaskDelay  │ ──► │ metro idle  │
│    _beat    │     │  (flash ms) │     │ + delay rest│

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 04  |  Step: ex04.c
rbdev run 04   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex04.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

#define BPM 120u
#define FLASH_MS 80u

static unsigned period_ms(unsigned bpm)
{
    if (bpm < 40u) bpm = 40u;
    return 60000u / bpm;   /* 120 BPM → 500 ms */
}

void app_main(void)
{
    unsigned period = period_ms(BPM);
    bsp_init();
    metro_init();
    metro_start();
    metro_set_bpm(BPM);

    for (;;) {
        metro_flash_beat();
        vTaskDelay(pdMS_TO_TICKS(FLASH_MS));
        metro_set_beat_idle();
        vTaskDelay(pdMS_TO_TICKS(period - FLASH_MS));
    }
}

How the code works

period_ms.
Converts BPM to milliseconds between beats (60000 / bpm). Clamp avoids divide-by-zero / insane tempos.

metro_start / metro_set_bpm.
Draw the face once; show the tempo on the big line.

Loop.
Flash the beat row, hold briefly, clear it, then sleep the rest of the period. vTaskDelay paces and yields — no audio yet.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — vTaskDelay for a period

Here delay is used as a crude period generator. That is fine for a visual demo. Later steps put work before a relative delay and the real period grows — that is the jitter lesson.

7. Checkpoint

  • Can compute ms from BPM
  • Beat flash looks steady
  • Can say what vTaskDelay is doing

Step 02 — UI click.

Lab 04 / Step 02 — UI click

Track: device-labs · Project: labs/device-labs

rbdev run 04
# step switch in ex04-metronome.c:  ex04_step02_start

Source: labs/device-labs/ex04-metronome/steps/step-02-ui-click/README.md

Device lab: Metronome
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p
Depends on: Step 01

1. What this step must deliver

  • Same BPM face.
  • Each beat: flash + beep_play(BEEP_CLICK) then vTaskDelay(period).
  • Click is in the same loop as the UI.

Success: you hear a click roughly at 120 BPM (exact timing comes next).

2. Problems this step faces

  • beep_play blocks for the click duration.
  • Relative delay starts after that work → real period = work + delay.

3. Design approach

ChoiceWhy
Beep in UI loopSimple; sets up the jitter lesson
vTaskDelay(period) after beepLooks like “wait one period” — incomplete

Architecture

The same UI loop now plays audio; blocking work sits before the relative delay.

┌─────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐
│ metro_flash │ ──► │  beep_play  │ ──► │ vTaskDelay  │
│             │     │  (blocks)   │     │  (period)   │
└─────────────┘     └─────────────┘     └─────────────┘

Timing

Beat-to-beat spacing grows because the delay starts after the click finishes.

beat n                 beat n+1 (late)

  .--flash+beep---------.-- delay(period) --.
  .<----- target period ------------------->.
  .<-------- actual exceeds period -------->.

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 04  |  Step: ex04.c
rbdev run 04   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex04.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

void app_main(void)
{
    unsigned period = period_ms(120);
    bsp_init();
    metro_init();
    metro_start();
    metro_set_bpm(120);

    for (;;) {
        metro_flash_beat();
        (void)beep_play(BEEP_CLICK, &(beep_params_t){
            .gain = 16u, .ms = 25u, /* quiet; still blocks — timing lesson */
        });
        metro_set_beat_idle();
        vTaskDelay(pdMS_TO_TICKS(period));
    }
}

How the code works

Beat body.
Flash, play a short click, clear the flash.

beep_play.
Does not return until the click finishes. That time is not subtracted from the following delay.

vTaskDelay(period).
Sleeps period ms from now — after the beep — so the gap between beat starts is longer than period. Step 03 measures that slip.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — relative delay after work

CallStarts counting from
vTaskDelay(N)Now (after previous work)

So:

  loop period ≈ time(beep + draw) + N

not N alone. That is acceptable for a first click, wrong for a metronome.

7. Checkpoint

  • Hear click + see flash
  • Can explain why period ≥ beep + delay
  • Ready to measure jitter

Step 03 — Feel jitter.

Lab 04 / Step 03 — Feel jitter

Track: device-labs · Project: labs/device-labs

broken on purpose

rbdev run 04
# step switch in ex04-metronome.c:  ex04_step03_start

Source: labs/device-labs/ex04-metronome/steps/step-03-feel-jitter/README.md

Device lab: Metronome
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p
Depends on: Step 02

1. What this step must deliver

  • Same UI-loop click as step 02.
  • Measure time between loop starts vs expected period.
  • Show last and max absolute error (ms) on the LCD.

Success: jitter numbers are clearly above ~0 (often tens of ms from the click).

2. Problems this step faces

  • Need proof that relative delay after work slips.
  • Students should see numbers, not only “it feels off.”

3. Design approach

ChoiceWhy
Stamp xTaskGetTickCount at loop startActual period between beats
|actual − expected|Simple jitter metric
Keep beep + vTaskDelaySame broken pacing as step 02

Architecture

Measure at loop start; then do work and a relative delay — the slip shows up in the numbers.

┌─────────────┐
│ loop start  │── tick stamp ──► abs(err) ms ──► LCD jitter
└──────┬──────┘
       │
       ▼
┌─────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐
│ flash+beep  │ ──► │ vTaskDelay  │──► next loop start (late)
│   (work)    │     │  (relative) │
└─────────────┘     └─────────────┘

Timing

Each beat adds beep time on top of the nominal period.

intended:  .---- period ----.---- period ----.
actual:    .work.-- period --.work.-- period --.
                 ^ error grows vs expected edge

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 04  |  Step: ex04.c
rbdev run 04   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex04.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

for (;;) {
    now = xTaskGetTickCount();
    if (!first) {
        actual_ms = (int)((now - prev) * portTICK_PERIOD_MS);
        err = actual_ms - (int)period;
        last_j = abs(err);
        if (last_j > max_j) max_j = last_j;
        metro_set_jitter(last_j, max_j);
    }
    first = 0;
    prev = now;

    metro_flash_beat();
    (void)beep_play(BEEP_CLICK, NULL);
    metro_set_beat_idle();
    vTaskDelay(pdMS_TO_TICKS(period));  /* relative — slips */
}

How the code works

Stamp at loop start.
prev / now mark when each beat attempt begins.

Error.
actual_ms should equal period for a perfect metronome. With beep + vTaskDelay(period), actual is larger → |err| shows up as jitter.

Display.
metro_set_jitter(last, max) keeps a running worst case so the slip is hard to miss.

The lesson.
FreeRTOS delay did what you asked. You asked for “sleep period after work,” not “wake every period.” Step 04 switches to vTaskDelayUntil.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — measuring with ticks

PieceRole
xTaskGetTickCount()Timestamp
(now - prev) * portTICK_PERIOD_MSElapsed ms
Expected periodIdeal BPM spacing

Resolution is one tick (often 1 or 10 ms). That is enough to see click-induced slip.

7. Checkpoint

  • Max jitter clearly > 0
  • Can point at beep + relative delay as the cause
  • Ready for vTaskDelayUntil

8. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
Jitter always 0Measuring after delay instead of at loop start
Huge jitterFirst iteration skipped via first flag?

Step 04 — Tick task.

Lab 04 / Step 04 — Tick task + vTaskDelayUntil

Track: device-labs · Project: labs/device-labs

rbdev run 04
# step switch in ex04-metronome.c:  ex04_step04_start

Source: labs/device-labs/ex04-metronome/steps/step-04-tick-task/README.md

Device lab: Metronome
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p
Depends on: Step 03

1. What this step must deliver

  • tick_task owns the beat: click + vTaskDelayUntil.
  • ui_task only displays BPM / flash / jitter.
  • Jitter drops vs step 03 (beat schedule compensates for click time).

Success: clicks sound steadier; max jitter much smaller.

2. Problems this step faces

  • UI work must not define the beat schedule.
  • Need absolute-period pacing, not “delay from now.”

3. Design approach

ChoiceWhy
tick_task at higher priorityBeat work first
vTaskDelayUntil(&last, period)Wake on a fixed schedule
Shared volatile statsUI reads beat count / jitter
UI never calls beep_playDisplay only

Architecture

The beat timetable lives in tick_task; UI only mirrors state.

┌─────────────┐   beat++   ┌─────────────┐   poll   ┌─────────────┐
│  tick_task  │ ─────────► │  g_stats    │ ◄─────── │   ui_task   │
│ DelayUntil  │            │ beat/jitter │          │ flash / LCD │
└─────────────┘            └─────────────┘          └─────────────┘

Sequence

tick_task              ui_task
    |                      |
    |-- beep_play          |
    |-- DelayUntil         |
    |-- beat++             |
    |                      |-- sees beat change
    |                      |-- flash + jitter
    |-- beep_play          |-- vTaskDelay (UI only)
    |-- DelayUntil         |
    |                      |

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 04  |  Step: ex04.c
rbdev run 04   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex04.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

static metro_stats_t g_stats;
static unsigned g_period_ms;

static void tick_task(void *arg)
{
    TickType_t last_wake = xTaskGetTickCount();
    TickType_t period_ticks = pdMS_TO_TICKS(g_period_ms);

    for (;;) {
        /* ... measure jitter from prev wake ... */
        g_stats.beat++;
        (void)beep_play(BEEP_CLICK, &(beep_params_t){
            .gain = 16u, .ms = 25u, .echo = 5u, .release = 10u,
        });

        /* next wake = last_wake + period (not "now + period") */
        vTaskDelayUntil(&last_wake, period_ticks);
    }
}

static void ui_task(void *arg)
{
    unsigned seen = 0;
    metro_init();
    metro_start();
    metro_set_bpm(120);
    for (;;) {
        if (g_stats.beat != seen) {
            seen = g_stats.beat;
            metro_flash_beat();
            metro_set_jitter(g_stats.last_j, g_stats.max_j);
            vTaskDelay(pdMS_TO_TICKS(60));
            metro_set_beat_idle();
        } else {
            vTaskDelay(pdMS_TO_TICKS(50));
        }
    }
}

void app_main(void)
{
    bsp_init();
    g_period_ms = 60000u / 120u;
    (void)xTaskCreate(tick_task, "tick", 4096, NULL, 6, NULL);
    (void)xTaskCreate(ui_task, "ui", 4096, NULL, 5, NULL);
}

How the code works

app_main.
Sets the period, starts tick (prio 6) then ui (prio 5).

tick_task.
On each beat: update jitter stats, bump g_stats.beat, play click, then vTaskDelayUntil. The API advances last_wake by period_ticks each call, so time spent in beep_play is subtracted from the sleep (as long as work < period).

ui_task.
Watches g_stats.beat. On change, flash and show jitter. UI delays do not move the beat schedule.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — vTaskDelayUntil

Relative vs absolute

APIMeaning
vTaskDelay(N)Sleep N ticks from now
vTaskDelayUntil(&last, N)Sleep until last + N; then last += N

Timing

vTaskDelay (relative)          vTaskDelayUntil (absolute)
|-- work --|-- N --|           |-- work --|-- N-work --|
|<--- N + work --->|           |<-------- N ---------->|

Arguments

ArgumentRole
&last_wakeIn/out: previous wake time (must init once)
period_ticksIdeal spacing between wakes

Limits

If work ever takes longer than one period, you cannot catch up by sleeping negative time — you will run late / skip. Keep the click short vs BPM period.

Mental model

Delay = nap from now.
DelayUntil = meet a timetable.

7. Checkpoint

  • Can contrast vTaskDelay vs vTaskDelayUntil
  • Jitter much lower than step 03
  • Can say why UI delay no longer shifts the beat

8. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
Still high jitterStill using vTaskDelay in tick — switch to Until
No flashUI not seeing g_stats.beat updates
Build error on DelayUntilInclude freertos/task.h; init last_wake

ChoiceWhy

┌─────────────┐  write bpm  ┌─────────────┐  read bpm  ┌─────────────┐
│  ui_task    │ ──────────► │  g_stats    │ ─────────► │  tick_task  │
│ BOOT edge   │             │    .bpm     │            │ DelayUntil  │

       │
       ▼
┌─────────────┐
│bsp_buttons  │
│ BOOT bit1   │
└─────────────┘

Sequence

ui_task              tick_task
    |                      |
    |-- BOOT edge          |

    |-- metro_set_bpm      |-- read g_stats.bpm
    |                      |-- beep_play
    |                      |-- DelayUntil
    |                      |

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 04  |  Step: ex04.c
rbdev run 04   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex04.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

#define BOOT_BTN_MASK 2u
#define BPM_MIN 60u
#define BPM_MAX 180u
#define BPM_STEP 10u

static void tick_task(void *arg)
{
    TickType_t last_wake = xTaskGetTickCount();
    for (;;) {
        unsigned period = period_ms(g_stats.bpm);
        TickType_t period_ticks = pdMS_TO_TICKS(period);
        /* ... jitter measure ... */
        g_stats.beat++;
        (void)beep_play(BEEP_CLICK, &(beep_params_t){
            .gain = 16u, .ms = 25u, .echo = 5u, .release = 10u,
        });
        vTaskDelayUntil(&last_wake, period_ticks);
    }
}

static void ui_task(void *arg)
{
    uint32_t boot_was = 0;
    for (;;) {
        if (boot_pressed(&boot_was)) {
            unsigned bpm = g_stats.bpm + BPM_STEP;
            if (bpm > BPM_MAX) bpm = BPM_MIN;
            g_stats.bpm = bpm;
            g_stats.max_j = 0;
            metro_set_bpm(bpm);
        }
        /* ... flash on g_stats.beat change + show jitter ... */
    }
}

void app_main(void)
{
    bsp_init();
    g_stats.bpm = 120u;
    (void)xTaskCreate(tick_task, "tick", 4096, NULL, 6, NULL);
    (void)xTaskCreate(ui_task, "ui", 4096, NULL, 5, NULL);
}

How the code works

tick_task.
Each iteration re-reads g_stats.bpm, converts to period_ticks, clicks, then vTaskDelayUntil. New tempos apply on the following cycles.

ui_task.
BOOT edge bumps BPM (wrap), clears max jitter, updates the banner. Still never calls beep_play. Beat flash follows g_stats.beat as in step 04.

app_main.
Seed BPM at 120; create tick then UI.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — reuse + timetable

No new API beyond step 04. The lesson is product control on top of an absolute tick:

PieceRole
vTaskDelayUntilSteady beat
Shared bpmUI writes, tick reads
BOOT edgeHuman tempo control

Relative vTaskDelay stays out of the beat path on purpose.

7. Checkpoint

  • BOOT steps BPM (not PWR)
  • Jitter stays low after tempo change
  • Can name Lab 04’s 3 FreeRTOS features

8. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
No BPM changePress BOOT
Wild jitter onceExpected for one interval after change
No clickTick task / volume / BOARD

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

void app_main(void)
{
    float t = 0.0f;
    float ax;

    bsp_init();
    scope_init();
    scope_start();
    scope_set_status("fake wave");

    for (;;) {
        ax = 8.0f * imu_sim_sin(2.0f * IMU_SIM_PI * 0.7f * t);
        scope_push(ax);
        t += 0.033f;
        vTaskDelay(pdMS_TO_TICKS(33));
    }
}

How the code works

scope_start.
Opens the display and mounts a one-channel HISTORY chart (ring buffer inside lab_scope, capacity sized for a full-width pane).

Fake wave.
Each loop computes a soft sine (helper from imu_sim.h, no libm) and scope_pushes it into the history ring. The plot grows left→right; once full, it shifts so the newest sample stays at the right edge.

vTaskDelay(33).
~30 Hz pace + yield. Step 02 swaps the fake value for IMU/imu_sim_fill.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — vTaskDelay

Same pace + yield lesson. Later steps stress what happens when you push/draw much faster than the panel can comfortably update.

  • Wave fills then shifts on the LCD (HISTORY)
  • Can name scope_push
  • Knows delay sets ~30 Hz

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

void app_main(void)
{
    float acc[3], gyro[3];
    float t = 0.0f;
    uint32_t rng = 1u;
    int real, was_real = -1;

    bsp_init();
    scope_init();
    scope_start();

    for (;;) {
        real = (bsp_imu_read(acc, gyro) == 0);
        if (!real) {
            imu_sim_fill(acc, gyro, t, &rng);
            t += 0.02f;
        }
        if (real != was_real) {
            was_real = real;
            scope_set_live(real);
        }

        scope_push(acc[0]);
        /* Contract: keep short. 100 Hz draw is the break in step 03. */
        vTaskDelay(pdMS_TO_TICKS(20));
    }
}

How the code works

IMU / sim.
Same live-vs-sim pattern as Labs 00–02. Status shows which path.

scope_push(acc[0]).
One horizontal accel sample per loop → one strip column.

Delay 20 ms.
~50 Hz is enough to look live without saturating draw work.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — single-loop contract

Sense + draw may share one loop only if every pass stays short.

AllowedNot yet
IMU + one chart push @50 HzSample+draw every 10 ms

  • Strip tracks motion / sim
  • Can name the short-loop contract
  • Ready to break it at 100 Hz

ChoiceWhy (for the lesson)

Architecture

Sample and full chart draw share one loop at 10 ms — UI work dominates.

┌────────────────────────────────┐
│   single loop @100 Hz (BAD)    │
│ IMU + scope_push every 10 ms   │
│      chart draw too heavy      │
└────────────────────────────────┘

Timing

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

scope_set_status("100 Hz draw BAD");

for (;;) {
    /* ... bsp_imu_read / imu_sim_fill ... */
    /*
     * INTENTIONAL BUG: draw on every 100 Hz sample in this task.
     */
    scope_push(acc[0]);
    vTaskDelay(pdMS_TO_TICKS(10));
}

How the code works

Same structure as step 02, only the period shrinks to 10 ms and status advertises the bad rate.

The bug.
Each scope_push redraws the HISTORY chart. Doing that 100 times per second in the only task makes the device feel stuck even though FreeRTOS is running the delays.

Contrast.
You still want ~100 Hz samples for vibration detail — but the UI should draw slower. Step 04 splits those rates with a queue.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — rate mismatch

JobComfortable rate
IMU sample100 Hz OK
Chart draw~30 Hz OK

One loop forcing both rates to 100 Hz is the architecture smell — not a kernel bug.

7. Checkpoint

  • Felt lag / stutter vs step 02
  • Can say sample rate ≠ draw rate
  • Ready for a sample queue

Step 04 — Split + queue.

Lab 05 / Step 04 — Split + sample queue

Track: device-labs · Project: labs/device-labs

rbdev run 05
# step switch in ex05-vibration-scope.c:  ex05_step04_start

Source: labs/device-labs/ex05-vibration-scope/steps/step-04-split-queue/README.md

Device lab: Vibration Scope
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p
Depends on: Step 03

1. What this step must deliver

  • sensor_task samples ~100 Hz and xQueueSends ax.
  • ui_task runs ~30 Hz: drains the queue and scope_pushes.
  • Chart looks smooth again while sensing stays fast.

Success: live strip without the step-03 slog.

2. Problems this step faces

  • Need different rates for produce vs draw.
  • Need a short backlog (not Lab 01’s “latest only”).

3. Design approach

ChoiceWhy
Queue length 64Hold ~0.6 s at 100 Hz if UI pauses briefly
Send(..., 0)Drop rather than block sensor
UI drain with Receive(..., 0)Batch-draw then delay ~33 ms
Sensor prio ≥ UIPrefer filling the buffer

Architecture

┌─────────────┐   Send    ┌─────────────┐  drain   ┌─────────────┐
│sensor_task  │ ────────► │ sample Q    │ ───────► │  ui_task    │
│ IMU @100Hz  │           │  ax x64     │ @30Hz    │ rb_chart    │
└─────────────┘           └─────────────┘          └─────────────┘

Sequence

sensor_task         sample Q          ui_task
      |                |                 |
      |-- read IMU     |                 |
      |-- Send ------->|                 |
      |-- delay 10ms   |                 |
      |-- Send ------->|                 |
      |                |-- Receive batch>|
      |                |                 |-- scope_push x N
      |                |                 |-- delay 33ms
      |                |                 |

Rejected: len-1 overwrite (Lab 01) — UI would see only the newest sample per frame and throw away vibration detail between draws.

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 05  |  Step: ex05.c
rbdev run 05   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex05.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

static QueueHandle_t g_samples;

static void sensor_task(void *arg)
{
    float ax;
    for (;;) {
        /* ... read imu/sim into acc ... */
        ax = acc[0];
        (void)xQueueSend(g_samples, &ax, 0);
        vTaskDelay(pdMS_TO_TICKS(10));
    }
}

static void ui_task(void *arg)
{
    float ax;
    scope_init();
    scope_start();
    for (;;) {
        while (xQueueReceive(g_samples, &ax, 0) == pdTRUE) {
            scope_push(ax);
        }
        vTaskDelay(pdMS_TO_TICKS(33));
    }
}

void app_main(void)
{
    bsp_init();
    g_samples = xQueueCreate(64, sizeof(float));
    (void)xTaskCreate(sensor_task, "sensor", 4096, NULL, 6, NULL);
    (void)xTaskCreate(ui_task, "ui", 4096, NULL, 5, NULL);
}

How the code works

app_main.
Creates a float queue (64 slots), then sensor (prio 6) and UI (prio 5).

sensor_task.
~100 Hz: read IMU/sim, Send ax with timeout 0 (never block on a full queue).

ui_task.
Each frame: drain all pending samples into scope_push, then vTaskDelay(33) (~30 Hz). Drawing happens in bursts of several columns, not 100 separate full-loop iterations with long stalls.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — stream queue vs Lab 01/02

Lab 01 tiltLab 02 alarmLab 05 scope
Item{ax,ay}eventfloat ax
Length1 overwrite4 events64 samples
GoalLatest onlyA few cuesShort backlog

Drain pattern

while (xQueueReceive(q, &ax, 0) == pdTRUE) {
    scope_push(ax);
}

Timeout 0 = non-blocking. Empty → stop draining. That batches work for one UI frame.

Mental model

Fast producer → queue → slower consumer.
Different rates, one buffer.

7. Checkpoint

  • Smooth strip vs step 03
  • Can explain why length > 1
  • Can explain drain + 30 Hz delay

8. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
Empty chartQueue create / Send path
Still laggyUI still pushing inside sensor — split tasks
Build target wrongset-target esp32s3

Lab 05 / Step 05 — Drop stats

Track: device-labs · Project: labs/device-labs

finished device

rbdev run 05
# step switch in ex05-vibration-scope.c:  ex05_step05_start

Source: labs/device-labs/ex05-vibration-scope/steps/step-05-drop-stats/README.md

Device lab: Vibration Scope
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p
Depends on: Step 04

1. What this step must deliver

  • Same producer/consumer split as step 04.
  • Shorter queue (32) so overflows are visible under load.
  • Status line: approx input Hz + cumulative drops.
  • Sensor Send failure increments drops.

Success: status like in~100Hz drop N; chart still usable.

2. Problems this step faces

  • Silent Send(..., 0) failures hide backlog pressure.
  • Need a simple way to see produce rate vs loss.

3. Design approach

ChoiceWhy
Count failed SendDrops are explicit
Count successful pushesApprox Hz over 1 s
Queue len 32Easier to observe drops than 64

Architecture

Non-blocking Send exposes backlog pressure as counters.

┌─────────────┐   Send    ┌─────────────┐  drain   ┌─────────────┐
│sensor_task  │ ────────► │ sample Q    │ ───────► │  ui_task    │
│ IMU @100Hz  │           │    x32      │ @30Hz    │ show stats  │
└─────────────┘           └─────────────┘          └─────────────┘

Sequence

sensor_task         sample Q           ui_task
      |                 |                 |
      |-- read IMU      |                 |
      |-- Send -------->|                 |
      |-- pushed++      |                 |
      |-- Send fail     |                 |
      |-- drops++       |                 |
      |                 |-- Receive batch>|
      |                 |                 |-- scope_push
      |                 |                 |-- status line
      |                 |                 |

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 05  |  Step: ex05.c
rbdev run 05   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex05.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

typedef struct {
    volatile unsigned drops;
    volatile unsigned pushed;
} scope_stats_t;

static scope_stats_t g_stats;

/* sensor: */
if (xQueueSend(g_samples, &ax, 0) != pdTRUE) {
    g_stats.drops++;
} else {
    g_stats.pushed++;
}

/* ui, once per second: */
hz = g_stats.pushed - last_pushed;
last_pushed = g_stats.pushed;
scope_set_status("%uHz d%u", hz, g_stats.drops);  /* title is short */

void app_main(void)
{
    bsp_init();
    g_samples = xQueueCreate(32, sizeof(float));
    (void)xTaskCreate(sensor_task, "sensor", 4096, NULL, 6, NULL);
    (void)xTaskCreate(ui_task, "ui", 4096, NULL, 5, NULL);
}

How the code works

Sensor.
On each sample, try non-blocking Send. Failure means the queue was full — increment drops instead of blocking (which would slow sensing).

UI.
Same drain + 30 Hz delay as step 04. About once a second, compute how many successful pushes happened since last report (hz) and show cumulative drops.

app_main.
Smaller queue (32) than step 04 so students can provoke / observe loss without waiting forever.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — full queues are data

PolicyBehavior
Send(..., portMAX_DELAY)Sensor blocks — can distort timing
Send(..., 0) + countKeep sensing; admit loss

Drops are not “FreeRTOS failing” — they are backpressure. Fix by faster UI, larger queue, or accepting loss for live scopes.

Lab 05 feature recap

FeatureRole
vTaskDelay100 Hz sensor / 30 Hz UI
TasksProducer vs consumer
QueueSample stream between them

7. Checkpoint

  • Status shows Hz and drops
  • Can explain why Send(..., 0) can fail
  • Can contrast Lab 01 overwrite vs this backlog queue

8. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
Always drop 0Queue large / UI keeping up — OK
Hz ~0Sensor not running / wrong board
Wrong chip GPIOsset-target esp32s3

What the finished device must do

  • LCD lists running tasks (name, state, priority, stack high-water mark).

vTaskDelay paces the UI refresh. This lab is about seeing the system, not about a new IPC primitive.

Step 02 — Live names.

Lab 06 / Step 02 — Live names

Track: device-labs · Project: labs/device-labs

rbdev run 06
# step switch in ex06-task-monitor.c:  ex06_step02_start

Source: labs/device-labs/ex06-task-monitor/steps/step-02-live-names/README.md

Device lab: Task Monitor
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p
Depends on: Step 01

1. What this step must deliver

  • Create worker tasks load_a / load_b.
  • UI task snapshots the system ~2 Hz and shows live task names.
  • Title shows how many tasks the snapshot returned.

Success: names change from fake strings to real kernel names (including IDLE / ui).

2. Problems this step faces

  • Hard-coded names lie when you add tasks.
  • Need a snapshot API, not a continuous trace.

3. Design approach

ChoiceWhy
uxTaskGetSystemStateFills a TaskStatus_t array once
Array length 16Enough for this lab; teach sizing
Refresh every 500 msReadable; avoids hammering the kernel
Extra load_* tasksMake the list interesting

Architecture

┌─────────────┐                 ┌─────────────┐  draw  ┌──────────┐
│ load_a/b    │                 │  ui_task    │ ─────► │  mon_*   │
│ Blocked     │                 │ SystemState │  2 Hz  │  names   │
└─────────────┘                 └─────────────┘        └──────────┘

Sequence

ui_task                    kernel
 |                           |
 |-- GetSystemState -------->|
 |<- TaskStatus array -------|
 |-- mon_set_row             |
 |-- vTaskDelay 500ms ------>|

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 06  |  Step: ex06.c
rbdev run 06   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex06.c)

Needs CONFIG_FREERTOS_USE_TRACE_FACILITY=y (already in sdkconfig.defaults).

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

TaskStatus_t snap[16];
UBaseType_t n = uxTaskGetSystemState(snap, 16, NULL);
mon_clear_rows();
for (i = 0; i < n && row < MON_ROWS; i++) {
    mon_set_row(row++, "%.8s", snap[i].pcTaskName);
}
mon_set_title("names %u", (unsigned)n);

How the code works

Workers.
load_task only delays — it stays Blocked most of the time, but still appears in the snapshot.

uxTaskGetSystemState.
Writes up to SNAP_MAX entries. Return value = how many tasks were copied. If the system has more tasks than the array, the list is truncated (size the array for your product).

UI pacing.
Snapshot + draw, then vTaskDelay(500). A snapshot every loop with no delay makes the UI feel busy for no teaching gain.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — system state snapshot

APIRole
uxTaskGetSystemState(buf, len, total_runtime)Fill TaskStatus_t array
pcTaskNameName string (truncated on LCD)
total_runtimeOptional; pass NULL here

Rejected here: parsing vTaskList text — awkward for LCD columns. Prefer the structured array.

Mental model: photo of the task list, not a video of the scheduler.

7. Checkpoint

  • Live names include ui and load_*
  • Can explain truncated array risk
  • Can point at GetSystemState in the sketch

8. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
Link / compile error on GetSystemStateTrace facility in sdkconfig; fullclean + set-target
Only 1–2 namesWorkers not created

Step 04 — Stack HWM.

Lab 06 / Step 04 — Stack HWM

Track: device-labs · Project: labs/device-labs

rbdev run 06
# step switch in ex06-task-monitor.c:  ex06_step04_start

Source: labs/device-labs/ex06-task-monitor/steps/step-04-stack-hwm/README.md

Device lab: Task Monitor
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p
Depends on: Step 03

1. What this step must deliver

  • Each row adds stack high-water mark (minimum free stack, in words).
  • Format: name SP HWM e.g. load_a B3 380.
  • Workers use different stack sizes / a small pad so HWM differs.

Success: HWM numbers are visible and make sense vs create stack depth.

2. Problems this step faces

  • Stack overflow is silent until crash — need a margin metric.
  • Students confuse HWM with “stack used right now.”

3. Design approach

ChoiceWhy
uxTaskGetStackHighWaterMark(handle)Classic FreeRTOS teaching API
Also show snapshot usStackHighWaterMarkSame photo; either works
Larger stack on load_bHWM should usually be higher

Architecture

┌─────────────┐  handle   ┌─────────────┐
│ TaskStatus  │ ────────► │ Stack HWM   │
│  xHandle    │           │ free words  │
└─────────────┘           └──────┬──────┘
                                 │
                                 ▼
                          ┌─────────────┐
                          │ mon row HWM │
                          └─────────────┘

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 06  |  Step: ex06.c
rbdev run 06   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex06.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

hwm = uxTaskGetStackHighWaterMark(snap[i].xHandle);
mon_set_row(row, "%.5s %c%u %u",
            snap[i].pcTaskName,
            state_ch(snap[i].eCurrentState),
            (unsigned)snap[i].uxCurrentPriority,
            (unsigned)hwm);

How the code works

HWM meaning.
High-water mark = the smallest amount of free stack the task has ever had (in words). Lower HWM → closer to overflow historically.

Not “current free.”
It does not climb back up after a deep call returns. It is a worst-case minimum free watermark.

Worker pad.
volatile uint32_t pad[32] burns some stack once so numbers are not identical noise across tasks.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — stack high-water mark

APIRole
uxTaskGetStackHighWaterMark(task)Min free words left for that task
TaskStatus_t.usStackHighWaterMarkSame idea inside a system snapshot

Units: words (on Xtensa, typically 4 bytes/word — confirm in docs if you convert to bytes).

Mental model: how close did this task ever get to the cliff?

7. Checkpoint

  • HWM column visible
  • Can explain “minimum free,” not current free
  • Ready for queue depth + paging

8. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
HWM 0Stack nearly exhausted — enlarge create size
Same HWM everywhereDifferent create sizes / pad array

Lab 06 / Step 05 — Queue depth + page

Track: device-labs · Project: labs/device-labs

finished device

rbdev run 06
# step switch in ex06-task-monitor.c:  ex06_step05_start

Source: labs/device-labs/ex06-task-monitor/steps/step-05-queue-depth/README.md

Device lab: Task Monitor
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p
Depends on: Step 04

1. What this step must deliver

  • Demo queue length 8; fill_task sends often; UI drains slowly.
  • Title shows Q waiting/len and page number.
  • BOOT advances through pages of 3 task rows.
  • Rows still show name / state / prio / HWM.

Success: queue count moves; BOOT flips pages of the task list.

2. Problems this step faces

  • Only three LCD rows — need paging for longer lists.
  • Queue fill is invisible without MessagesWaiting / spaces.

3. Design approach

ChoiceWhy
uxQueueMessagesWaitingHow many items queued
uxQueueSpacesAvailableFree slots; waiting + spaces == length
BOOT edgeSame button pattern as Labs 00 / 04
Snapshot then page sliceDraw rows [page*3 .. page*3+2]

Architecture

┌─────────────┐  Send   ┌──────────┐  depth   ┌─────────────┐
│ fill_task   │ ──────► │ demo Q   │ ───────► │ title Qn/L  │
└─────────────┘         │ len 8    │          └─────────────┘
                        └────┬─────┘
                             │ drain 1 / refresh
┌─────────────┐  snapshot    ▼
│ load / ui   │ ──────► ui_task ──► mon rows (page)
└─────────────┘              ▲
                             │ BOOT edge
                        ┌────┴────┐
                        │  BOOT   │
                        └─────────┘

Sequence

fill           queue            ui               boot
 |              |              |                |
 |-- Send ----->|              |                |
 |              |<- waiting ---|                |
 |              |<- Receive ---|                |
 |              |              |<- edge --------|
 |              |              |-- page++       |

4. Build

cd labs/device-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 06  |  Step: ex06.c
rbdev run 06   # from labs/device-labs (step still in ex06.c)

5. Student sketch (main/main.c)

waiting = uxQueueMessagesWaiting(g_demo_q);
spaces  = uxQueueSpacesAvailable(g_demo_q);
mon_set_title("Q%u/%u p%u", waiting, waiting + spaces, page + 1);

start = page * MON_ROWS;
for (row = 0; row < MON_ROWS; row++) {
    i = start + row;
    if (i >= snap_n) break;
    /* draw snap[i] ... */
}

How the code works

Producer.
fill_task uses non-blocking xQueueSend(..., 0) every 100 ms so a full queue does not stall the filler forever.

Consumer.
UI takes one item per refresh — slower than fill — so waiting climbs toward 8, then wobbles as drain catches bursts.

Paging.
g_page indexes groups of three. BOOT rising edge increments; wrap when past the last page.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — queue depth

APIRole
uxQueueMessagesWaitingItems currently in the queue
uxQueueSpacesAvailableEmpty slots left
Sum of bothQueue length (when no concurrent change mid-read)

Contrast Lab 05: there the queue carried samples. Here the queue exists so you can see depth on the monitor.

Mental model: depth is a gauge; the task list is a paged table.

7. Checkpoint

  • Title Q…/… moves over time
  • BOOT changes which three tasks show
  • Can explain waiting vs spaces

8. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
Q always 0fill_task not created / Send failing
BOOT does nothingEdge detect / BOOT_BTN_MASK bit1
Page emptysnap_n small — still create workers

ex01 — Mutex Beep

Track: special-labs · Project: labs/special-labs

│  Step 01    │ ──► │  Step 02    │ ──► │  Step 03    │
│ two voices  │     │ overlap BAD │     │ mutex guard │
│ staggered   │     │ garbled beep│     │ clean audio │
└─────────────┘     └─────────────┘     └─────────────┘

Lab 06 — Task Monitor · labs/README.md

S1 / Step 01 — Two voices

Track: special-labs · Project: labs/special-labs

rbdev run 01
# step switch in ex01-mutex-beep.c:  ex01_step01_start

Source: labs/special-labs/ex01-mutex-beep/steps/step-01-two-voices/README.md

Special: Mutex Beep
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p

1. Deliver

  • Two tasks (hi / lo) each play a tone and update an LCD line.
  • Delays are staggered so overlaps are rare.

Success: alternating-ish beeps; LCD shows who plays.

2. Problems

  • Need a baseline with two callers of one speaker before the race is obvious.

3. Design

ChoiceWhy
Equal priority tasksFair schedule
Stagger start + long gapHide the race for now
 
┌─────────┐     ┌───────────┐
│ hi_task │ ──► │ beep_play │
└─────────┘     └───────────┘

┌─────────┐     ┌───────────┐
│ lo_task │ ──► │ beep_play │
└─────────┘     └───────────┘
     staggered in time

cd labs/special-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 01  |  Step: ex01.c

5. Sketch

xTaskCreate(hi_task, "hi", 4096, NULL, 4, NULL);
xTaskCreate(lo_task, "lo", 4096, NULL, 4, NULL);
/* each: beep_play(...); vTaskDelay(700); */

How it works

Two tasks share beep_play with no lock. Long gaps make it sound fine — step 02 removes the gap.

6. FreeRTOS — tasks only

xTaskCreate + vTaskDelay. Mutex arrives in step 03.

Step 02 — Overlap.

S1 / Step 02 — Overlap (broken)

Track: special-labs · Project: labs/special-labs

broken on purpose

rbdev run 01
# step switch in ex01-mutex-beep.c:  ex01_step02_start

Source: labs/special-labs/ex01-mutex-beep/steps/step-02-overlap-crash/README.md

Special: Mutex Beep
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p

1. Deliver

  • Same two tasks, short delays, longer tones.
  • Overlap on purpose — audio sounds wrong / stepped on.

Success: you can hear (and name) the shared-resource bug.

2. Problems

  • beep_play is blocking and owns the speaker path — two callers corrupt it.

3. Design

ChoiceWhy
Short vTaskDelay(120)Force concurrent entry
No lockMake the failure obvious
 
┌─────────┐          ┌───────────┐
│ hi_task │ ────────►│ beep_play │
└─────────┘          │ UNSAFE    │
┌─────────┐          │ overlap   │
│ lo_task │ ────────►│           │
└─────────┘          └───────────┘

4. Build

cd labs/special-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 01  |  Step: ex01.c
rbdev run 01   # from labs/special-labs (step still in ex01.c)

5. Sketch

Same as step 01, but delays ~120 ms and tones ~180 ms — they collide.

How it works

Scheduler can switch while beep_play runs (or start the other task’s beep while the first still owns the hardware path). Result: garbled audio.

6. FreeRTOS — why a lock

ToolFit?
Notify“Event happened” — no mutual exclusion
QueueCan serialize requests (Lab 02) — heavier here
Mutex“Only one task in the critical section”

Step 03 — Mutex guard.

S1 / Step 03 — Mutex guard

Track: special-labs · Project: labs/special-labs

finished device

rbdev run 01
# step switch in ex01-mutex-beep.c:  ex01_step03_start

Source: labs/special-labs/ex01-mutex-beep/steps/step-03-mutex-guard/README.md

Special: Mutex Beep
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p

1. Deliver

  • xSemaphoreCreateMutex guards every beep_play.
  • Aggressive timing from step 02, but audio stays clean.
  • LCD still shows hi/lo.

Success: overlapping requests, serialized playback.

2. Problems

  • Need exclusive access without redesigning as an audio queue (Lab 02).

3. Design

ChoiceWhy
Mutex Take / GiveCritical section around beep
portMAX_DELAY TakeWait for the other voice to finish
 
┌─────────┐  Take   ┌───────┐  Give
│ hi/lo   │ ──────► │ mutex │ ─────► speaker
└─────────┘         │ beep  │
                    └───────┘

Sequence

hi               mutex            lo
 |                 |               |
 |-- Take -------->|               |
 |-- beep_play     |               |
 |-- Give -------->|               |
 |                 |<- Take -------|
 |                 |  beep_play    |
 |                 |<- Give -------|

4. Build

cd labs/special-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 01  |  Step: ex01.c
rbdev run 01   # from labs/special-labs (step still in ex01.c)

5. Sketch

g_beep_mu = xSemaphoreCreateMutex();
xSemaphoreTake(g_beep_mu, portMAX_DELAY);
beep_play(...);
xSemaphoreGive(g_beep_mu);

How it works

Take blocks until the mutex is free. Only one task runs beep_play. Give unlocks. Always Give after Take (even if you add error paths later).

6. FreeRTOS in depth — mutex

APIRole
xSemaphoreCreateMutexCreate lock (initial “given”)
xSemaphoreTakeEnter critical section
xSemaphoreGiveLeave

vs binary semaphore: mutex knows its owner (priority inheritance on many ports). Use mutex for shared data/hardware; binary sem for signal.

vs Lab 02 queue: queue serializes messages to one audio task. Mutex serializes callers of a shared function. Both valid; this lab teaches the lock.

Step 02 — ISR busy.

S2 / Step 02 — ISR busy (broken)

Track: special-labs · Project: labs/special-labs

broken on purpose

rbdev run 02
# step switch in ex02-boot-isr.c:  ex02_step02_start

Source: labs/special-labs/ex02-boot-isr/steps/step-02-isr-busy/README.md

Special: Boot ISR
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p

1. Deliver

  • GPIO0 NEGEDGE ISR increments a hit counter and busy-waits.
  • UI tries to tick every 100 ms.

Success: while ISR spins, ui ticks stalls — system feels frozen.

2. Problems

  • Long work in ISR blocks scheduling (and other interrupts).

3. Design

ChoiceWhy
Real gpio_isr_handler_addHonest ISR path
Busy loop in ISRVisible freeze (safer demo than calling beep_play in ISR)
 
┌──────────┐  edge  ┌──────────────┐
│ BOOT GPIO│ ─────► │ boot_isr     │
└──────────┘        │ BUSY LOOP BAD│
                    └──────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
                    ui_task starved

4. Build

cd labs/special-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 02  |  Step: ex02.c
rbdev run 02   # from labs/special-labs (step still in ex02.c)

Press/hold BOOT and watch ui ticks pause.

5. Sketch

static void IRAM_ATTR boot_isr(void *arg)
{
    g_isr_hits++;
    for (i = 0; i < 800000; i++) { } /* BAD */
}

How it works

While the ISR runs, the scheduler does not run your UI task. That is why ui ticks freezes. Step 03 keeps the ISR tiny.

6. FreeRTOS — ISR rules (preview)

Allowed (typical): set flag, give sem/NotifyGiveFromISR, queue send FromISR.
Not allowed here: vTaskDelay, beep_play, long loops, LCD draws.

Step 03 — ISR notify.

S2 / Step 03 — ISR notify

Track: special-labs · Project: labs/special-labs

finished device

rbdev run 02
# step switch in ex02-boot-isr.c:  ex02_step03_start

Source: labs/special-labs/ex02-boot-isr/steps/step-03-isr-notify/README.md

Special: Boot ISR
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p

1. Deliver

  • ISR only vTaskNotifyGiveFromISR (+ yield if needed).
  • UI task debounces, updates count, notifies audio.

Success: BOOT feels snappy; ui path stays responsive.

2. Problems

  • Need deferred work without polling forever (optional) and without ISR bloat.

3. Design

ChoiceWhy
NotifyGiveFromISRTiny ISR wake
Debounce in taskNeeds time APIs / logic unfit for ISR
 
BOOT ──► isr ──NotifyGiveFromISR──► ui_task ──NotifyGive──► audio

Sequence

GPIO           isr             ui              audio
 |              |               |                |
 |-- edge ----->|               |                |
 |              |-- FromISR --->|                |
 |              |               |-- debounce     |
 |              |               |-- LCD          |
 |              |               |-- Give ------->|
 |              |               |                |-- beep

4. Build

cd labs/special-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 02  |  Step: ex02.c
rbdev run 02   # from labs/special-labs (step still in ex02.c)

5. Sketch

vTaskNotifyGiveFromISR(g_ui, &hp);
if (hp == pdTRUE) {
    portYIELD_FROM_ISR();
}

How it works

FromISR variants are the only notify/queue/sem APIs safe in an ISR. pxHigherPriorityTaskWoken + portYIELD_FROM_ISR switches sooner to the woken task when it outranks the interrupted one.

6. FreeRTOS in depth — FromISR

APIRole
vTaskNotifyGiveFromISRIncrement notify count from ISR
portYIELD_FROM_ISRRequest switch if higher-prio task woken
ulTaskNotifyTake in taskWait for the ISR signal

Mental model: ISR shouts; task does the work.

Step 02 — High-prio hog.

S3 / Step 02 — High-prio hog (broken)

Track: special-labs · Project: labs/special-labs

broken on purpose

rbdev run 03
# step switch in ex03-priority-starve.c:  ex03_step02_start

Source: labs/special-labs/ex03-priority-starve/steps/step-02-high-prio-hog/README.md

Special: Priority Starve
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p

1. Deliver

  • hog at priority 6, tight loop, no delay.
  • ui at priority 4.

Success: LCD freezes (or barely updates); you name priority starvation.

2. Problems

  • Ready higher-prio task that never blocks owns the CPU.

3. Design

┌──────────┐  prio6  busy forever
│ hog_task │ ─────────────────────► CPU
└──────────┘
┌──────────┐  prio4  ready but starved
│ ui_task  │ ─────────────────────► frozen LCD
└──────────┘

4. Build

cd labs/special-labs
# Lab: rbdev run 03  |  Step: ex03.c
rbdev run 03   # from labs/special-labs (step still in ex03.c)

5. How it works

Preemptive FreeRTOS always runs the highest-priority ready task. A hog at 6 that never calls a blocking API never lets UI at 4 run.

S3 / Step 03 — Yield fix

Track: special-labs · Project: labs/special-labs

finished device

# step switch in ex03-priority-starve.c:  ex03_step03_start

Source: labs/special-labs/ex03-priority-starve/steps/step-03-yield-fix/README.md

Special: Priority Starve
Boards: ws28p or es3c28p

  • Hog runs below UI priority and vTaskDelay(1) after a work burst.
  • UI ticks smoothly; work counter still climbs.

  • Need CPU-bound work that still cooperates with the scheduler.

ChoiceWhy
Hog prio 3 < UI 5UI wins when both ready
vTaskDelay(1) in hogEven if prios were equal/wrong, blocking lets others run
 
┌──────────┐  prio5
│ ui_task  │ ─────────► LCD alive
└──────────┘

│ hog_task │ ─────────► work climbs
└──────────┘

Sequence

hog                 scheduler           ui
 |                     |                 |
 |-- busy burst ------>|                 |
 |-- Delay(1) -------->|                 |
 |                     |-- run ui ------>|
 |                     |<- Delay 100 ----|
 |<- wake -------------|                 |

/* hog: work burst; vTaskDelay(1); */

FreeRTOS in depth

IdeaMeaning
PriorityHigher ready task always wins
BlockingDelay / queue / notify Take → not ready → others run
StarvationReady high-prio task never blocks → lower prio never runs

Mental model: priority picks the winner; blocking gives others a turn.

APIRole
xEventGroupCreateBitfield object

ex00 — task creation

Track: rtos-labs · Project: labs/rtos-labs

rbdev run 00

Source: labs/rtos-labs/ex00-task-creation/README.md

Goal: understand how to create tasks, how two tasks run together, and how the scheduler switches them.

This is the first FreeRTOS exercise on purpose. Before queues, mutexes, or priorities, you need a solid picture of what a task is and who decides which one runs. The LCD is only a window. Each task owns one rb_term pane so you can see both loops advancing at different rates.

┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐
│ blink (cyan)   │  │ count (yellow) │
│ blink #0 c0    │  │ count #0 c1    │
│ blink #1 c0    │  │ count #1 c0    │
│ every 500 ms   │  │ every 1000 ms  │
└────────────────┘  └────────────────┘

cN is xPortGetCoreID() — which CPU ran that wake. With plain xTaskCreate there is no pin, so c0 / c1 may appear on either pane and can change over time.

Enable in main/main.c with ex00_start().

1. Learning objectives

IdeaIn this exercise
Create a taskxTaskCreate(fn, name, stack, arg, prio, handle)
MultitaskingTwo infinite loops — each looks like it has the CPU
SchedulerPicks who runs; vTaskDelay blocks so a peer can run
Task statesRunning → vTaskDelay → Blocked → wake → Ready → Running
Which core?xPortGetCoreID() — no affinity → may be 0 or 1

Success: both panes keep scrolling; blink advances about twice as fast as count. Each line shows cN. Cores may match or differ; either is correct without pinning.

What this exercise is not: forcing a core (xTaskCreatePinnedToCore — see ex01), priority design, or LCD widgets as the topic. The UI is evidence.

2. Mental model — what is a task?

A FreeRTOS task is a function that never returns (normally), plus:

  • its own stack
  • its own saved registers / program counter when it is not Running
  • a priority the scheduler uses when choosing who runs
  • an optional name and handle for debugging
┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                 task TCB                 │
│  state, priority, name, stack pointer    │
└───────────────────┬──────────────────────┘
                    │
                    ▼
┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              private stack               │
│  locals, call frames, ISR nesting room   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────┘

Analogy we use in reviews: a task is a thread that the RTOS owns. It is not a C++ thread object, not a Linux process, and not “a function you call when you feel like it.” Once created, it lives until you delete it (or the system resets).

ESP-IDF note: app_main itself runs in a task created by the startup code. When ex00_start returns, that does not stop blink/count — they are separate tasks. The idle task (and other system tasks) keep running too.

3. How to create a task

(void)xTaskCreate(pane_task, "blink", 3072, &blink, 4, NULL);
/*                 |          |       |      |      |  |
 *                 fn         name    stack  arg  prio handle
 */
ArgumentMeaning hereField tip
pane_taskFunction that becomes the task body (void fn(void *))One function can back many tasks if arg differs
"blink"Name (debugger / vTaskList / monitor)Keep short; FreeRTOS truncates long names
3072Stack depth in words (StackType_t), not bytesOn Xtensa, words are 4 bytes → ~12 KiB here
&blinkPassed as pvParameters into pane_taskMust outlive the task (see Tips)
4Priority (same for both tasks in this exercise)Higher number = higher priority in FreeRTOS
NULLWe do not keep a TaskHandle_tPass a handle when you need delete / notify later

ex00_start returns after creating both tasks. The tasks keep running under the scheduler; creation is the last FreeRTOS step in this function.

Return value — check it in real firmware

BaseType_t ok = xTaskCreate(...);
if (ok != pdPASS) {
    /* out of heap for TCB/stack — do not pretend the task exists */
}

In classroom demos we often cast to void. In shipping code, always check pdPASS. Silent failure looks like “my task never runs” for hours.

Where does the task run? (xPortGetCoreID)

xTaskCreate does not take a core id. On ESP-IDF SMP that means no affinity (tskNO_AFFINITY): the task may Run on core 0 or core 1, and the scheduler may migrate it later.

ex_terms_log(cfg->term, cfg->fg, "%s #%lu c%d", cfg->name,
             (unsigned long)n, (int)xPortGetCoreID());
What you seeMeaning
Always c0Fine — scheduler kept you on core 0 for now
Mix of c0 / c1Fine — no pin; migration is allowed
Want a fixed coreUse xTaskCreatePinnedToCore (ex01)

Field tip: never assume “I created it from app_main on core 0, so the task lives on core 0 forever.” Print xPortGetCoreID() when core matters. Hope is not affinity.

4. How multitasking looks

Both tasks share the same function (pane_task) but different args (pane, name, color, period). That is still two tasks — two stacks, two program counters, two independent n locals.

┌────────────┐  create   ┌────────────┐
│ ex00_start │ ────────► │ blink task │  left pane, 500 ms
└────────────┘           └────────────┘
┌────────────┐  create   ┌────────────┐
│ ex00_start │ ────────► │ count task │  right pane, 1000 ms
└────────────┘           └────────────┘

On the LCD you watch two independent counters. That is the evidence that multitasking works — not that two CPUs are busy all the time.

Same function, two tasks — why professionals do this

ApproachWhen it is good
One fn, many tasks + argIdentical behavior, different config (this lab)
Separate blink_task / count_taskBodies diverge; clearer names in backtraces
One task that does everythingUsually worse — harder timing, harder debugging

Reuse the function when the control flow is the same. Split functions when the job is different (sensor vs UI vs protocol).

5. How the scheduler works (this exercise)

FreeRTOS on ESP-IDF is preemptive: a higher-priority Ready task can preempt a lower-priority Running task. In this exercise both tasks have the same priority, so you mainly see blocking make room for the peer.

The important call is vTaskDelay:

  Running              Blocked               Ready             Running
┌──────────┐ vTaskDelay ┌──────────┐  tick  ┌──────────┐ pick ┌──────────┐
│  blink   │ ─────────► │  blink   │ ─────► │  blink   │ ───► │  blink   │
│  logs #n │            │  waiting │        │ wants CPU│      │  logs    │
└──────────┘            └──────────┘        └──────────┘      └──────────┘

While blink is Blocked, the scheduler can Run count (or idle).

CallEffect
pdMS_TO_TICKS(ms)Convert milliseconds → ticks for this build
vTaskDelay(ticks)This task Blocked; scheduler runs someone else
No delay in a tight loopTask stays Running and starves same/lower peers

Blink delays 500 ms; count delays 1000 ms. While one is Blocked, the other (or idle) can Run. That is cooperative yielding on top of a preemptive kernel.

Tick rate: delays are quantized to the RTOS tick (often 100 Hz / 10 ms on ESP-IDF defaults). Asking for 1 ms may become one tick. Do not design hard real-time edges with vTaskDelay alone — later labs cover vTaskDelayUntil and hardware timers.

Not yet: locking a task to one core (xTaskCreatePinnedToCore in ex01), priority design, mutexes, notifications.

6. Read the code (ex00.c)

  1. ex_terms_begin(1, 2) — UI helper only (see common/ex_terms.h).
  2. Fill two pane_cfg_t structs (arg for each task).
  3. Two xTaskCreate calls — this is the FreeRTOS lesson (no core pin).
  4. Each task: log with xPortGetCoreID() → vTaskDelay → forever.

Notice blink and count are static. That is deliberate — see Tips.

7. Tips and tricks (from the lab bench)

Tip — arg lifetime is a classic bug

void bad_start(void)
{
    pane_cfg_t cfg;          /* stack of the creator */
    cfg.period_ms = 500;
    xTaskCreate(pane_task, "blink", 3072, &cfg, 4, NULL);
    /* bad_start returns → cfg is gone → pane_task reads garbage */
}

Do this instead: static storage, file-scope globals, or heap that you own for the life of the task. Passing &local into xTaskCreate is one of the most common student (and junior) bugs.

Tip — tasks must not “return”

Falling off the end of a task function is undefined / asserts in many configs. Use for (;;) or call vTaskDelete(NULL) if the work is truly finished.

Tip — stack size is in words, and “enough” is measured

3072 is not magic. Too small → stack overflow (canary / panic). Too large → wasted RAM (many small MCUs die here first). In real projects:

  1. Start with a generous size while bringing a feature up.
  2. Use high-water mark APIs / Lab 06-style monitoring.
  3. Trim with evidence, not vibes.

Leave headroom for printf-style formatting, deep call chains, and ISRs that nest on the current stack (port-dependent).

Tip — always leave a blocking point in periodic work

A task that only burns CPU:

for (;;) {
    do_work();   /* no vTaskDelay / queue / semaphore */
}

will starve same-priority peers (time-slicing helps only if enabled and only among Ready tasks of equal prio — do not rely on it for structure). Design the loop as work → wait, not work forever.

Tip — names are free debugging

"blink" / "count" show up in crashes, uxTaskGetSystemState, and monitors. "t1" / "t2" waste your future self’s time. Prefer verbs or subsystem names: imu_sample, ui_draw, net_tx.

Tip — log the core when placement matters

xPortGetCoreID() is cheap evidence. In bring-up we often log it once at task start and periodically (as here). If a bug is “works on my desk, fails with Wi-Fi,” compare cores before you rewrite the algorithm.

Tip — same priority is a teaching tool, not a product default

Equal priority + delays is the clearest way to see multitasking. In products you usually separate:

Kind of workTypical priority idea
Hard deadlines / controlHigher
UI / loggingLower
Background housekeepingLowest (or idle hooks)

Do not invent a unique priority for every task “just in case.” Fewer levels, clear rules.

Tip — check creation; log failures early

Out-of-memory at xTaskCreate is common when someone copies a demo and spawns ten fat stacks. Fail loud at boot, not with a blank pane three minutes later.

Tip — one responsibility per task (scale this habit now)

This exercise is tiny, but the habit matters: blink does not also parse UART; count does not also drive I2C. When a task owns two unrelated jobs, timing bugs become undebuggable. Split early.

8. Focus / avoid / consider

Focus on

  • Exact meaning of each xTaskCreate argument
  • Task states: Running / Ready / Blocked
  • Why vTaskDelay is both a timer and a yield
  • Evidence on the LCD: two rates + cN from xPortGetCoreID()
  • No pin ⇒ core is observed, not promised

Avoid

  • Passing pointers to creator-stack locals as arg
  • Tight loops with no blocking API
  • Returning from a task body
  • Assuming stack size is in bytes
  • Assuming “created on core 0” means “always runs on core 0”
  • Treating “it works on my board” as proof of stack safety

Consider

  • What happens if blink’s period becomes 1 and count’s stays 1000?
  • What happens if you delete the delay in one task only?
  • Where does the idle task fit when both are Blocked?
  • How would you stop one task later (TaskHandle_t + vTaskDelete)?
  • How would you make cN never change? (ex01 — pin the task)

9. Common mistakes (field notes)

SymptomLikely causeWhat we check first
One pane never movesPeer stuck in tight loop / higher prio hogDelays and priorities
Crash right after createStack too small / bad arg pointerStack size; static cfg
Both rates look identicalSame period_ms by copy-pasteConfig structs
Task “never started”xTaskCreate failed (!= pdPASS)Heap; return value
Weird strings / colorsarg pointed at dead stackLifetime of pane_cfg_t
Works in debug, dies in releaseStack closer to the edgeHigh-water mark

10. Checkpoint

  • Can name every xTaskCreate argument and its unit for stack
  • Can explain why both panes move without a second CPU busy forever
  • Can say what vTaskDelay does to task state
  • Can explain why blink / count configs are static
  • Can predict what happens if you remove the delay
  • Can say why checking pdPASS matters in production
  • Can explain what cN means and why it may change without a pin

11. Experiments (try on the board)

  1. Change blink to 100 ms — relative rates should jump.
  2. Remove vTaskDelay from one task only — watch the other pane starve or stutter.
  3. Give count priority 5 and blink 4 — with both still delaying, both run; remove count’s delay and blink suffers (preview of priority).
  4. Watch cN for a minute — note whether either pane ever flips core.
  5. Add a third pane: ex_terms_begin(1, 3) + a third xTaskCreate.
  6. Intentionally pass a stack-local cfg (then fix it) — feel the bug once so you recognize it forever.
  7. Move to ex01 — pin cores and see cN freeze.

12. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
Blank LCDbsp_init in app_main; correct -DBOARD=
Only one pane logsCheck both xTaskCreate calls and both periods
Build cannot find ex_terms.hBuild from labs/rtos-labs; after adding common/ or any new .c, run idf.py -DBOARD=ws28p reconfigure (or -DBOARD=es3c28p) then build
Linker undefined reference to a new .cSame: reconfigure for your board, then build — see rtos-labs README
Linker / heap assert at createLower stack sizes or free heap; check pdPASS

13. Next

Keep this picture in your head: create → Ready → Run → Block → Ready. Every later FreeRTOS object (queue, semaphore, notification) is another way to move a task between those states on purpose.

ex01 — task affinity

Track: rtos-labs · Project: labs/rtos-labs

rbdev run 01

Source: labs/rtos-labs/ex01-task-affinity/README.md

Goal: understand how to pin a task to a CPU core, how two cores run two tasks at once, and what affinity does not give you for free.

Builds on ex00 (xTaskCreate + vTaskDelay). Here both panes use the same period; the new evidence is the core id printed on every line.

┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐
│ core0 (cyan)   │  │ core1 (yellow) │
│ pin=0 on=0     │  │ pin=1 on=1     │
│ core0 #0 c0    │  │ core1 #0 c1    │
│ every 500 ms   │  │ every 500 ms   │
└────────────────┘  └────────────────┘

Enable in main/main.c:

#include "ex01.h"
/* ... */
ex01_start();

(Comment out the previous exNN_start — only one exercise at a time.)

1. Learning objectives

IdeaIn this exercise
AffinityxTaskCreatePinnedToCore(..., coreID)
Prove the pinxPortGetCoreID() inside the task
Dual schedulerESP32-S3 has two cores → two Ready/Running sets
Shared worldHeap, FreeRTOS objects, and globals are still shared

Success: left pane always shows c0, right pane always shows c1. Counters advance together at ~500 ms.

What this exercise is not: IPC between cores, spinlocks, or “make my app twice as fast.” Pinning is placement, not a speed cheat code.

2. Mental model — two cores, one firmware image

ESP32-S3 is a dual-core Xtensa chip. FreeRTOS (ESP-IDF SMP) runs a scheduler on each core. A pinned task may only be Running on its chosen core.

┌─────────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────┐
│       core 0        │     │       core 1        │
│  scheduler + tasks  │     │  scheduler + tasks  │
│  (this lab: core0)  │     │  (this lab: core1)  │
└─────────────────────┘     └─────────────────────┘
            \                     /
             \   shared RAM      /
              \  heap / TCBs    /
               \_______________/

Analogy: two kitchens in one restaurant. Affinity says which kitchen a cook works in. The pantry (RAM) is still shared — so you still need discipline when two cooks touch the same shelf (later: mutex / queue).

Unpinned tasks: xTaskCreate (or pin with tskNO_AFFINITY) lets the scheduler migrate the task between cores. That is fine for many jobs; pin when you care about locality, ISR pairing, or isolating heavy work.

3. How to pin a task

(void)xTaskCreatePinnedToCore(pane_task, "core0", 3072, &core0, 4, NULL, 0);
/*                            |          |       |      |      |  |     |
 *                            fn         name  stack  arg  prio  h  core
 */
ArgumentMeaning here
First fiveSame idea as xTaskCreate (see ex00)
handleNULL here; keep one if you need delete / notify
coreID0 or 1 on ESP32-S3; or tskNO_AFFINITY

Read the live core

int core = (int)xPortGetCoreID();

Call this inside the task. Logging the pin you requested is not enough — on=cN proves where the scheduler actually ran you.

Return value

Same rule as ex00: real firmware checks pdPASS. Creation can still fail (heap). Affinity does not create extra RAM.

4. How this differs from ex00

┌────────────┐  pin 0   ┌────────────┐
│ ex01_start │ ───────► │ core0 task │  always on CPU0
└────────────┘          └────────────┘
┌────────────┐  pin 1   ┌────────────┐
│ ex01_start │ ───────► │ core1 task │  always on CPU1
└────────────┘          └────────────┘
ex00ex01
xTaskCreate (no affinity)xTaskCreatePinnedToCore
Different periodsSame period (fair visual)
Lesson: multitasking + delayLesson: which core runs you

Both still use vTaskDelay so each core’s idle task can run when the worker is Blocked.

5. Scheduler picture (dual core)

On each core, the familiar state machine still holds:

  Running              Blocked               Ready             Running
┌──────────┐ vTaskDelay ┌──────────┐  tick  ┌──────────┐ pick ┌──────────┐
│  core0   │ ─────────► │  core0   │ ─────► │  core0   │ ───► │  core0   │
│  logs    │            │  waiting │        │ wants CPU│      │  logs    │
└──────────┘            └──────────┘        └──────────┘      └──────────┘

The new fact: core1 can be Running at the same wall-clock time as core0. That is true parallelism for two Ready tasks pinned apart — something a single core can only fake by switching.

ESP-IDF default: protocol / Wi-Fi often live on a specific core. Your app tasks share those cores with system work. Pinning everything to core 1 “to leave core 0 alone” is a strategy — measure before you believe it.

6. Read the code (ex01.c)

  1. ex_terms_begin(1, 2) — UI only.
  2. Two pane_cfg_t with pin_core 0 and 1 (static lifetime — same tip as ex00).
  3. Two xTaskCreatePinnedToCore calls.
  4. Each loop logs cN from xPortGetCoreID().

7. Tips and tricks (from the lab bench)

Tip — pin for a reason, not for decoration

Good reasons: keep a fast control loop away from Wi-Fi; run UI on one core and DSP on the other; match a peripheral ISR’s core. Bad reason: “dual core means I must pin everything.” Unpinned can be simpler and still correct.

Tip — shared data is still shared

Two cores writing the same global without sync is a data race — often worse than on one core because true overlap is more likely. Affinity is not a mutex. This lab avoids sharing mutable state on purpose.

Tip — LCD / drivers may live on one core

Logging from both cores into a display helper that takes a lock is OK if the lock is correct. If a driver is “core 0 only” by design, calling it from core 1 can deadlock or corrupt. Know your BSP. Our ex_terms_log path uses the display lock inside rb_term — fine for demos; still treat UI as a shared resource.

Tip — same stack / prio rules as ex00

Stack size is still in words. arg must still outlive the task. pdPASS still matters. Pinning does not fix a too-small stack.

Tip — tskNO_AFFINITY vs hard pin

ChoiceUse when
Pin 0 / 1You need a stable home core
tskNO_AFFINITYSoft real-time work that can migrate
Only xTaskCreateSame as no affinity on ESP-IDF

Tip — proving affinity in reviews

In code review we ask: “Where do you print / assert xPortGetCoreID()?” If the answer is nowhere, the pin is a hope, not a verified property.

Tip — load balance is not automatic magic

Pinning two light tasks to two cores looks great on this LCD. Pinning ten heavy tasks all to core 1 makes core 0 idle and core 1 late. Design the map; then measure (task monitor / runtime stats — later labs).

8. Focus / avoid / consider

Focus on

  • xTaskCreatePinnedToCore’s last argument
  • Reading xPortGetCoreID() every loop
  • “Two Running tasks at once” vs ex00’s time-slicing story

Avoid

  • Assuming pin doubles performance
  • Sharing writable globals across cores with no sync
  • Pinning every task “because the chip has two cores”
  • Forgetting system tasks already occupy both cores

Consider

  • What if both tasks pin to core 0? (Back to single-core contention.)
  • What if one task is unpinned? (May migrate; cN can change.)
  • Where should a future queue producer / consumer live?

9. Common mistakes

SymptomLikely causeCheck first
Both panes show c0Both pinned to 0 / create failedCreate calls; pdPASS
cN changes over timeUnpinned or wrong APILast arg of create
Crash in log from core 1Driver / lock assumptionsBSP thread safety
One pane deadCreate failed; bad arg lifetimeHeap; static cfg

10. Checkpoint

  • Can write xTaskCreatePinnedToCore from memory
  • Can explain pin vs xPortGetCoreID proof
  • Can say why shared RAM still needs care
  • Can contrast this lab with ex00 (affinity vs multitasking intro)

11. Experiments

  1. Pin both tasks to core 0 — both panes show c0; they take turns.
  2. Pin both to core 1 — same story on the other CPU.
  3. Use tskNO_AFFINITY for one task — watch whether cN ever flips.
  4. Drop one task’s vTaskDelay — that core stays busy; the other core’s pane still advances (true parallel contrast to ex00 starvation demos).

12. Troubleshooting

SymptomTry
Blank LCDbsp_init; -DBOARD=; only one exNN_start
Link error on xTaskCreatePinnedToCoreESP-IDF FreeRTOS / SMP target esp32s3
ex01.h not foundRebuild from labs/rtos-labs after adding sources

13. Next

You can place work on a core. Next skills: communicate safely between tasks (and cores) with queues / notifications, and design priorities so the important work wins without starving the rest.
